People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

mistakes from the migration and start feeling like I let down the whole department, my family, and even the students I mentor, way beyond what probably makes sense.\n- key: S032\n  name: I don’t really notice myself finishing people’s sentences. If I’m supposed to just sit and watch a show, I start feeling physically uncomfortable, like I need to get up. I’ll pace around the house with my laptop, or I’ll suddenly start reorganizing the garage or rewriting project boards instead of just staying seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some weeks I feel really up, almost electric, like I can handle anything and I don’t want to slow down. But then something small, like a delay or my wife asking me a simple question, can set me off. I’ll snap because it feels like people are getting in my way on purpose, even if I know later that’s probably not fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice myself doing that a lot. I can talk more when I’m worked up or focused on something, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where people can’t get a word in. No one has really told me that directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or having trouble sitting still in most situations, like at work, at home, or in social settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly at home, but it can happen in other places too. I’ll end up pacing around with my laptop, or going off to reorganize the garage, or rewriting project boards instead of just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors caused problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s caused real problems. It’s like I can’t stop starting things—work plans, certification schedules, home network upgrades, even reaching out to old contacts. I’ll get locked in and push past normal limits, and then my wife or my director has to step in and tell me to stop or pull back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where you felt abnormally happy or irritable for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes. There are stretches, sometimes for weeks, where I feel almost electric—like I’m unstoppable and everything is moving too slowly around me. But it can flip into irritation really fast. Like if my wife asks a question or there’s some small delay, I can snap because it feels like people are trying to slow me down on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where these symptoms, such as restlessness and impulsivity, were not present or were significantly less severe?\n\nPatient: Yes. Like I used to look forward to grilling, climbing, playing music at church, helping mentor the robotics kids… and then for months I just don’t care. I’ll let messages sit unanswered and projects kind of fade out because I can’t make myself feel interested in them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes.
